Semi-local behaviour of non-local hypoelliptic equations: divergence form
Amélie Loher

Abstract
. We derive the Strong Harnack inequality for a class of hypoelliptic integro-differential equations in divergence form. Our result is semi-local, in the sense that we require the equation to hold globally in velocity. In fact, it is known that the Strong Harnack inequality fails in the kinetic case if the equation is merely satisfied in a bounded velocity domain [ 25 ] . This is in stark contrast to the case of parabolic equations, for which a local Strong Harnack inequality holds. In a first step, we derive a local bound on the non-local tail on upper level sets by exploiting the coercivity of the cross terms. In a second step, we perform a De Giorgi argument in L 1 L^{1} , since we control the tail term only in L 1 L^{1} . This yields a linear L 1 L^{1} to L ∞ L^{\infty} bound. Consequently, we prove polynomial upper bounds for s ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) s\in(0,1) and exponential lower bounds for s ∈ ( 1 2 , 1 ) s\in(\frac{1}{2},1) on the fundamental solution by adapting Aronson’s method to non-local hypoelliptic equations.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Problem formulation

We consider kinetic integro-differential equations of the form

(1)

\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=\mathcal{L}f+h,\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}},~x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},~v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},

where we assume
h=h(t,x,v)
is a real measurable scalar field and where
\mathcal{L}
is defined by

(2)

\mathcal{L}f(t,x,v):=\textrm{PV}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(t,x,w)-f(t,x,v)\big]K(t,x,v,w)\,{\rm d}w,

with a non-negative measurable kernel
K:{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
. Here,
PV
denotes the Cauchy principal value, that is

\displaystyle\textrm{PV}

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}K(t,x,v,w)\big[f(t,x,w)-f(t,x,v)\big]\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(v)}K(t,x,v,w)\big[f(t,x,w)-f(t,x,v)\big]\,{\rm d}w.

Given a solution to (
1
) in some bounded subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{1+d}
in time and space, and on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
in velocity, we derive a linear bound on the local supremum of this solution by its local infimum in the future.
Such a relation is known as the Strong Harnack inequality for solutions of (
1
). As a consequence, we deduce polynomial upper and exponential lower bounds (for
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
) on the fundamental solution of (
1
) away from the initial time.

Equation (
1
) is an integro-differential equation. The non-locality measures fluctuations in velocity for a local time and space. In particular, the non-local operator takes into account values of the function outside any local velocity domain. The regularity of solutions to (
1
) has first been investigated by Imbert-Silvestre
[
22
,
24
]
. In particular, it is known that super-solutions of (
1
) satisfy the Weak Harnack inequality,
[
22
, Theorem 1.6]
and
[
35
, Theorem 1.1]
, that is some local average of a power of the solution in a past cylinder is bounded by the local infimum in a future cylinder. This is even true for solutions in bounded velocity domains. The proof of the Weak Harnack inequality uses a gain of integrability in form of a bound on the supremum by the
L^{2}
norm of the solution (an
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
bound)
[
22
, Lemma 6.6]
,
[
35
, Lemma 4.1]
.
Combining the Weak Harnack with the
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
bound suggests that solutions of (
1
) satisfy the Strong Harnack inequality. Intuitively, however, it is not clear that we expect the supremum of a solution to (
1
) to be bounded by a local quantity, since the values in the whole velocity domain affect the local supremum. In fact, the Strong Harnack inequality fails for kinetic equations satisfied in bounded velocity domains
[
25
]
.
So far only a
nonlinear
relation between the supremum and the
L^{2}
norm has been established for (
1
) in
[
35
, Lemma 4.1]
, which can be made linear at the cost of
non-local tail terms
that precisely take into account function values of the solution outside the domain in which (
1
) holds. We refer the reader to
[
35
, Remark 4.2]
. As a consequence of this nonlinear
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
bound
[
35
, Lemma 4.1]
and the Weak Harnack inequality
[
35
, Theorem 1.1]
, we deduced a nonlinear upper bound on the local supremum, which we called the “Not-so-Strong Harnack inequality”
[
35
, Theorem 1.3]
. Here, however, we aim to establish a
linear

L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
bound
without tail terms
, so that we improve the “Not-so-Strong Harnack inequality”
[
35
, Theorem 1.3]
to a Strong Harnack inequality. We show that if (
1
) is satisfied for any
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, then the Strong Harnack inequality holds. Such a statement is of independent interest since it captures the local regularity of solutions to a class of hypoelliptic integro-differential equations, and moreover, it bears interesting consequences, such as quantitative bounds on the fundamental solution.

1.2.
Main results

In this paper, we capture the local behaviour of solutions to non-local hypoelliptic equations.
We derive the Strong Harnack inequality for solutions of (
1
), and consequentially, we deduce polynomial upper bounds and exponential lower bounds on the fundamental solution of (
1
).
The kernels of the non-local operator (
2
) that we are interested in would ideally correspond to the setting that was introduced in
[
39
]
and used in
[
22
,
24
,
35
]
: there the ellipticity class of the kernel was prescribed through physical considerations of gas dynamics modelled by the Boltzmann equation without cutoff assumptions.
In particular, the notion of ellipticity, introduced in
[
39
]
and reused here, generalises the commonly used pointwise bounds on the kernel of the non-local operator. The idea is to relate the non-local operator (
2
) to the fractional Laplacian in an average sense, see Subsection
1.6
below. In short, we assume a coercivity condition in integral form (
15
) and an integral upper bound (
16
). However, in contrast to the setting considered by
[
24
]
or
[
35
]
, we make a pointwise symmetry assumption stated in (
17
), which is the non-local analogue of local operators in divergence form.
Solutions to (
1
) of this class satisfy the Strong Harnack inequality,
provided that the equation is satisfied for any
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

Theorem 1

(Strong Harnack inequality)
.

Let
f\in L^{2}([-3,0]\times B_{1};H^{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
be a non-negative solution of (
1
)-(
2
) in
[-3,0]\times B_{1}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
in the sense of Definition
1
(which requires the solution to decay pointwisely in velocity almost everywhere in
[-3,0]\times B_{1}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
),
with a non-negative kernel
K
of order
2s\in(0,2)
that satisfies for given
\lambda_{0},\Lambda_{0}>0,
a coercivity condition on average (
15
), an upper bound on average (
16
), a pointwise divergence form symmetry (
17
) and, in case that
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
, a non-divergence form symmetry (
18
), and with a non-negative source term
0\leq h\in L^{\infty}([-3,0]\times B_{1}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. Then there is
C>0
depending on
s,d,\lambda_{0},\Lambda_{0}
such that for any
0<r_{0}<\frac{1}{6}
the Strong Harnack inequality is satisfied:

(3)

\sup_{\tilde{Q}^{-}_{\frac{r_{0}}{4}}}f\leq C\inf_{Q_{\frac{r_{0}}{4}}}f+C\left\|h\right\|_{L^{\infty}([-3,0]\times Q_{1}^{t})},

where
\tilde{Q}^{-}_{\frac{r_{0}}{4}}:=[-t_{3},-t_{2}]\times B_{\left(\frac{r_{0}}{4}\right)^{1+2s}}\times B_{\frac{r_{0}}{4}}
with
t_{2}=\frac{5}{2}r_{0}^{2s}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{2}\right)^{2s}\leq t_{3}=t_{2}+\left(\frac{r_{0}}{4}\right)^{2s}
, and
Q_{\frac{r_{0}}{4}}:=[-t_{1},0]\times B_{\left(\frac{r_{0}}{4}\right)^{1+2s}}\times B_{\frac{r_{0}}{4}}
with
t_{1}=\left(\frac{r_{0}}{4}\right)^{2s}
, see Figure
1
. In particular, any solution
f
of (
1
) with zero source term
h=0
satisfies

\sup_{\tilde{Q}^{-}_{\frac{r_{0}}{4}}}f\leq C\inf_{Q_{\frac{r_{0}}{4}}}f.

Q_{r}
Q_{\frac{r}{2}}
Q_{\frac{r}{4}}
Q^{-}_{r}
\tilde{Q}^{-}_{\frac{r}{2}}
\tilde{Q}^{-}_{\frac{r}{4}}
t
0
-t_{1}=-\left(\frac{r}{4}\right)^{2s}
-\left(\frac{r}{2}\right)^{2s}
-r^{2s}
-2r^{2s}
-t_{2}=-\frac{5}{2}r^{2s}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{r}{2}\right)^{2s}
-t_{3}=-\frac{5}{2}r^{2s}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{r}{2}\right)^{2s}-\left(\frac{r}{4}\right)^{2s}
-\frac{5}{2}r^{2s}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{r}{2}\right)^{2s}
-3r^{2s}

Figure 1.
The kinetic cylinder
Q_{r}
and its past cylinder
Q_{r}^{-}
for some
r>0
. The dashed vertical line represents the timeline. The blue cylinder
Q_{\frac{r}{2}}
and its corresponding past
\tilde{Q}^{-}_{\frac{r}{2}}
represent the domains appearing in the Weak Harnack inequality, Theorem
9
. The red cylinder
Q_{\frac{r}{4}}
and its corresponding past
\tilde{Q}^{-}_{\frac{r}{4}}
are the domains appearing in the Strong Harnack inequality, Theorem
1
.

Figure
1
illustrates a non-local to local bridge: we recover the same domains for the Harnack inequalities as in the local case
[
18
, Theorem 5]
.

As a consequence of Theorem
1
, we derive polynomial upper and, in case that
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
, exponential lower bounds on the fundamental solution of (
1
) with a non-negative kernel
K
satisfying (
15
)-(
18
),
provided that the fundamental solution exists
. To give sense to the next three theorems, we thus assume existence of a non-negative measurable function
J
, which is the fundamental solution of (
1
) connecting a given point
(t,x,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
with
(\tau,y,w)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
, in the sequel denoted by

J(t,x,v;\tau,y,w)=J(t-\tau,x-y-(t-\tau)w,v-w)=:J\big((\tau,y,w)^{-1}\circ(t,x,v)\big),

where
\circ
denotes the Galilean translation, that is
(t_{0},x_{0},v_{0})\circ(t,x,v)=(t_{0}+t,x_{0}+x+tv_{0},v_{0}+v)
, which respects the translation invariance of (
1
).
Moreover, we assume that the fundamental solution
J
has the following properties:

i.

For every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
there holds the normalisation

(4)

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}J(t,x,v)\,{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}v=1.

ii.

There holds
J\geq 0
, and for all
(t,x,v),(\tau,y,w)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
a form of symmetry

(5)

J\left((\tau,y,w)^{-1}\circ(t,x,v)\right)=J\left((\tau,x,v)^{-1}\circ(t,y,w)\right).

iii.

For any
0\leq\tau<\sigma<t<T
and any
(x,v),(y,w)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
the Chapman-Kolmogorov identity holds

(6)

\displaystyle J(t,x,v;\tau,y,w)

\displaystyle=J\left((\tau,y,w)^{-1}\circ(t,x,v)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}J\left((\sigma,\varphi,\xi)^{-1}\circ(t,x,v)\right)J\left((\tau,y,w)^{-1}\circ(\sigma,\varphi,\xi)\right)\,{\rm d}\varphi\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}J(t,x,v;\sigma,\varphi,\xi)J(\sigma,\varphi,\xi;\tau,y,w)\,{\rm d}\varphi\,{\rm d}\xi.

Then, we deduce, on the one hand, polynomial upper bounds.

Theorem 2

(Polynomial upper bounds on the fundamental solution)
.

Let
x,v,y_{0},w_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, and
0\leq\tau_{0}<\sigma<T
. Let
J
be the fundamental solution of (
1
) in
[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
with a vanishing source term
h=0
, with a pointwise polynomial velocity decay, and with a non-negative kernel
K
satisfying the coercivity assumption (
15
), the integral upper bound (
16
), the divergence form symmetry (
17
) and, in case that
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
, the non-divergence form symmetry (
18
). Assume
J
satisfies (
4
), (
5
) and (
6
). Then there exists
C>0
depending on
s,d,\lambda_{0},\Lambda_{0}
such that

(7)

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle(\sigma,x,v;\tau_{0},y_{0},w_{0})

\displaystyle\leq C(\sigma-\tau_{0})^{-\frac{2d(1+s)}{2s}}

\displaystyle\times\left[1+\frac{\max\left\{\left|v-w_{0}\right|^{2s},\left|x-y_{0}-(\sigma-\tau_{0})(v-w_{0})\right|^{\frac{2s}{1+2s}}\right\}}{\sigma-\tau_{0}}\right]^{-\frac{s}{4s}}.

On the other hand, we derive an exponential lower bound.

Theorem 3

(Exponential lower bounds on the fundamental solution)
.

Let
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
.
Let
x,v,y_{0},w_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
0<\tau_{0}<\sigma<T
.
Let
J
be the fundamental solution of (
1
) in
[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
with a vanishing source term
h=0
, with a pointwise polynomial velocity decay and with a kernel satisfying the coercivity assumption (
15
), the integral upper bound (
16
), the divergence form symmetry (
17
) and the non-divergence form symmetry (
18
). Then there exists a constant
C
depending on
s,d,\lambda_{0},\Lambda_{0}
such that

\displaystyle J(\sigma,x,v;\tau_{0},y_{0},w_{0})\geq{}

\displaystyle c(\sigma-\tau_{0})^{-\frac{d(1+s)}{s}}

\displaystyle\times\exp\left[-C\left(\frac{|v-w_{0}|}{(\sigma-\tau_{0})^{1/(2s)}}+\frac{|x-y_{0}-(\sigma-\tau_{0})w_{0}|}{(\sigma-\tau_{0})^{1+1/(2s)}}\right)^{\frac{2s}{2s-1}}\right].

Remark 1
.

i.

Note that in general, the fundamental solution has no explicit expression, not even in case of constant coefficients. The constant coefficient equation for (
1
), known as the fractional Kolmogorov equation, is given by

(8)

\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=(-\Delta_{v})^{s}f+h,

with source term
h\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
, and where
(-\Delta_{v})^{s}
denotes the fractional Laplacian, defined as

(9)

(-\Delta_{v})^{s}f(v)=C(d,s)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{f(v)-f(w)}{\left|v-w\right|^{d+2s}}\,{\rm d}w.

Equation (
8
) admits a fundamental solution, given through an implicit representation,

(10)

J_{0}(t,x,v)=C(d,s)t^{-\frac{2d(1+s)}{2s}}\mathcal{J}_{0}\left(\frac{x}{t^{1+\frac{1}{2s}}},\frac{v}{t^{\frac{1}{2s}}}\right),

where
\mathcal{J}_{0}
reads explicitly in Fourier variables

\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{0}(\varphi,\xi)=\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{1}\left|\xi-\tau\phi\right|^{2s}\,{\rm d}\tau\right).

In particular, the fundamental solution
J_{0}
satisfies (
4
), (
5
), (
6
), and moreover,
0\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}\in C^{\infty}
decays polynomially at infinity.

ii.

In case of rough coefficients, the existence of a fundamental solution operator has only recently been established by Auscher-Imbert-Niebel
[
4
]
for local and non-local hypoelliptic equations. In particular, their results are applicable to (
1
) with kernels satisfying (
15
)-(
17
).

iii.

In Theorem
2
and Theorem
3
, the time decay is optimal, as can be seen by comparing the exponents on time to (
10
). This is a consequence of the optimality of the linear
L^{1}
-
L^{\infty}
bound of Proposition
5
below.
However, neither the decay in space nor in velocity is optimal.

Concerning the exponential lower bound of Theorem
3
, it is not clear to us if we should expect polynomial lower bounds to hold without assuming a pointwise lower bound on the kernel of the non-local operator (
2
).

The polynomial upper bounds in Theorem
2
are sub-optimal in two ways. First, a minor improvement could possibly be achieved, if, in Aronson’s method (see Section
6.3
below), when we define the decay function
H
in (
89
), we replace
\max\{\left|v-w_{0}\right|,\left|x-y_{0}-tw_{0}\right|^{\frac{1}{1+2s}}\}
by the sum
\left|v-w_{0}\right|+\frac{\left|x-y_{0}-tw_{0}\right|}{t}
. It is conceivable that this leads to an improved decay in space to
\frac{s}{2}
instead of
\frac{s}{2(1+2s)}
.
Second, the most significant sub-optimality arises due to the way we treat the non-singular contribution of the fundamental solution in Aronson’s method (see Proposition
17
). To determine the decay of the non-singular part, we only use the integral upper bounds on the kernel (
16
). In the parabolic literature, the non-singular part is usually treated separately from the singular terms, using a technique referred to as Meyer’s decomposition
[
36
]
. Conditional to such a decomposition, see (
11
), we obtain the optimal decay at least in velocity, see Theorem
4
below.
Finally note that in the parabolic setting with kernels that are pointwisely bounded from above, the proof of Theorem
2
- which does not rely on Meyer’s decomposition - yields a decay that is not so far from optimal, see Appendix
B
.

For the statement of the conditional upper bounds, we denote by
J_{\rho}
the fundamental solution to the cutoff problem

\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=\mathcal{L}_{\rho}f,

where
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}f
is defined for any
\rho>0
as

\mathcal{L}_{\rho}f(t,x,v):=PV\int_{B_{\rho}(v)}(f(t,x,w)-f(t,x,v))K(t,x,v,w)\,{\rm d}w.

Then we show:

Theorem 4

(Conditional upper bounds on the fundamental solution)
.

Let
x,v,y_{0},w_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
0<\tau_{0}<\sigma<T
.
Let
J
be the fundamental solution of (
1
) in
[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
with a vanishing source term
h=0
, and with pointwise polynomial velocity decay.
We assume the kernel is coercive (
15
), satisfies integral upper bounds (
16
), and is symmetric in divergence form (
17
), and if
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
symmetric in non-divergence form (
18
).
If, moreover, there exists a constant
c>0
, such that for every
\rho>0
, there holds

(11)

J(\sigma,x,v;\tau_{0},y_{0},w_{0})\leq J_{\rho}(\sigma,x,v;\tau_{0},y_{0},w_{0})+c(\sigma-\tau_{0})\rho^{-(2d(1+s)+2s)},

then there exists
C>0
depending on
c,s,d,\Lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}
such that

(12)

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle(\sigma,x,v;\tau_{0},y_{0},w_{0})

\displaystyle\leq C(\sigma-\tau_{0})^{-\frac{2d(1+s)}{2s}}

\displaystyle\times\left[1+\frac{\max\left\{\left|v-w_{0}\right|^{2s},\left|x-y_{0}-(\sigma-\tau_{0})(v-w_{0})\right|^{\frac{2s}{1+2s}}\right\}}{\sigma-\tau_{0}}\right]^{-\frac{2d(1+s)+2s}{2s}}.

Remark 2
.

As outlined in Remark
1
, the major sub-optimality in Theorem
2
came from the non-singular contribution of the fundamental solution.
The condition in (
11
) precisely says that we can consider the non-singular part separately, so that we only need to use Aronson’s method on the singular term, for which we get the optimal decay at least in velocity.

In the parabolic literature, (
11
) is established (with a corresponding decay in
\rho
of order
d+2s
instead of
2d(1+s)+2s)
using parabolic maximum principles, see
[
17
,
16
]
, provided that the kernels are pointwisely bounded from above: there exists
\Lambda>0
such that

(13)

\forall v,w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\qquad K(v,w)\leq\Lambda\left|v-w\right|^{-(d+2s)}.

This assumption does not seem to be easily relaxable to integral upper bounds (
16
). Moreover, even if it is conceivable that the methods from the parabolic setting could be carried over, it is not clear that one would get the expected decay of
2d(1+s)+2s
on
\rho
.

1.3.
Contribution

On the one hand, Theorem
1
is the first Strong Harnack inequality for
hypoelliptic
equations without additional tail error term. We have announced the result in
[
34
]
, yet the derivation of the local tail bound
[
34
, Proposition 3.2]
that is sketched there contains an issue in Step 4, as it would require the supremum to be linear. The method proposed in this article circumvents this issue, by deriving a local tail bound on upper level sets, exploiting the sign of the cross terms, so that it can be directly incorporated into the local boundedness result, if we perform a De Giorgi iteration based on a gain of integrability in
L^{1}
.
Provided that the equation is satisfied for all
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, the Strong Harnack inequality improves the “Not-so-Strong Harnack inequality” previously obtained in
[
35
, Theorem 1.3]
, which stated a non-linear relation between the supremum in the past and the infimum in the present. This non-linear relation could be made linear at the prize of a non-local tail term, as was outlined in
[
35
, Remark 4.2]
, and thus the key to the Strong Harnack inequality (
3
) lies in capturing the behaviour of the non-local tail term, which we introduce for any
0<r<R
and any
v_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
as

(14)

\forall v\in B_{r}(v_{0}):\qquad\mathbf{T}(f;r,R,v_{0}):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{R}(v_{0})}f(w)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w.

The tail measures the non-singular part of the non-local diffusion operator
\mathcal{L}
defined in (
2
), see also
[
11
,
10
]
. In particular, we want to derive a bound on the tail in terms of local quantities only.
This was first achieved for parabolic equations in
[
29
, Theorem 1.9]
.
In contrast to the parabolic case, we prove a local tail bound
on upper level sets
, so that when we derive the energy estimate and the gain of integrability on level set functions, we can directly incorporate this local tail bound to obtain purely local estimates, which can then be iterated with a De Giorgi argument. This yields a
linear
relation between the
L^{\infty}
and the
L^{1}
norm of a
solution
(rather than merely a sub-solution) to (
1
) as stated in the following proposition, which in turn implies the Strong Harnack inequality, but which is of independent interest. We emphasise that for the tail bound to hold on level set functions, we require (
1
) to hold for all
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

Proposition 5

(
L^{1}
-
L^{\infty}
bound)
.

Let
R>0
and
z_{0}=(t_{0},x_{0},v_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
. Let
\mathcal{I}:=[-R^{2s}+t_{0},t_{0}]
and let
\Omega_{x}:=B_{R^{1+2s}}(x_{0}+(t-t_{0})v_{0})
.
Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{I}\times\Omega_{x};H^{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
be a non-negative solution of (
1
)-(
2
) in
\mathcal{I}\times\Omega_{x}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
in the sense of Definition
1
, with a kernel satisfying the coercivity condition (
15
), the upper bound (
16
) the symmetries (
17
), and (in case that
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
) (
18
), with an essentially bounded source term
h\geq 0
. Then for any
\delta\in(0,1)
there exists a large constant
C>1
depending on
\delta,s,d,\lambda_{0},\Lambda_{0}
such that

\textrm{for a.e. $z_{1}\in Q_{\frac{R}{8}}(z_{0})$:}\quad f(z_{1})\leq CR^{-(2d(1+s)+2s)}\int_{Q_{R}(z_{0})}f(z)\,{\rm d}z+\delta R^{2s}\left\|h\right\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{R}(z_{0}))}.

To the best of our knowledge, Proposition
5
is the first occurrence of the First De Giorgi Lemma for hypoelliptic integro-differential equations, where the supremum is bounded linearly by the
L^{1}
norm without any tail terms. Even in the non-local parabolic case, where the Strong Harnack inequality in
[
29
, Theorem 1.1]
naturally implies this linear
L^{1}
-
L^{\infty}
bound, such a statement has not been derived directly. In particular, a tail bound on upper level sets has not been deduced before, not even for parabolic equations.
The strength of this statement lies in the fact that it describes the local behaviour of a solution to a non-local equation in terms of local quantities only. The weakness, however, is that this result applies only to functions solving (
1
) for any
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Even though this limits the interest of the statement in itself, it does not affect the consequences on the fundamental solution derived in Theorem
2
and Theorem
3
. Moreover,
if we consider global solutions of the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation, then these satisfy pointwise decay estimates
[
19
]
, which seem to be sufficient to deduce a boundedness condition on the antisymmetric part of the kernel, which in turn is sufficient to derive the results of this paper for functions solving the Boltzmann equation for any
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. In short, concerning applications to a physically relevant kinetic model, the global in
v
assumption is justifiable.

Furthermore, or rather as a consequence of the local nature of Proposition
5
, Theorem
2
and Theorem
3
are the first occurrence of bounds on the fundamental solution of a kinetic integro-differential equation. Previous results have discussed two-sided estimates either for kinetic equations with a local diffusion operator with rough, but uniformly elliptic coefficients
[
33
,
32
,
3
]
, or for non-local parabolic equations, with kernels that satisfy pointwise upper bounds, divergence form symmetry (
17
) and coercivity as in (
15
), see
[
28
]
. In this paper, we use Aronson’s method, originally conceived for local parabolic equations
[
1
,
2
]
, and establish a strategy based thereon for kinetic integro-differential equations. This yields both the exponential lower bounds from Theorem
3
, and the polynomial upper bounds from Theorem
2
.

1.4.
Literature

1.4.1.
On the Strong Harnack inequality

For
local
equations, the Strong Harnack inequality simply follows from the Weak Harnack inequality combined with the
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
bound in the first De Giorgi Lemma
[
8
,
37
]
. We refer the reader to
[
15
, Theorem 4]
and
[
18
, Theorem 5]
for the proof of the Strong Harnack inequality for local kinetic equations.

For
non-local elliptic
equations, a Strong Harnack inequality was derived in
[
6
]
: first they show a local
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
bound on the solution with a non-local tail term, then they use the Weak Harnack inequality for elliptic non-local equations and a bound on the non-local tail term in terms of local quantities only, to obtain the full Harnack inequality.

For
non-local parabolic
equations, on the other hand, until recently there had only been probabilistic methods to prove a Strong Harnack inequality without any tail term
[
5
,
42
,
40
,
41
]
. In
[
5
,
42
]
, they consider pointwisely bounded kernels, and establish the Strong Harnack inequality using the corresponding stochastic jump process and heat kernel estimates. In
[
40
,
41
]
they consider nonlocal Dirichlet forms on doubling metric measure spaces, and characterise Harnack Inequalities in terms of conditions on the underlying geometry and assumptions on the kernel. The first
non-stochastic
approach for parabolic equations was derived in Kassmann-Weidner
[
29
]
. Their idea is to first improve the local boundedness result from
[
26
]
by bounding the local
L^{\infty}
norm of a solution by the sum of its local
L^{2}
norm and a non-local tail term in
L^{1}
. To this end, they introduce two strategies, both relying on a “tail corrector term”, essentially the
L^{1}
norm in time of the tail (
14
) with a negative sign, which has the property that it can be added to a solution to the equation such that the sum of these two terms remains a solution to the equation, and thus the tail corrector term has the effect of cancelling the tail terms arising through the equation. As a second step, they improve the Weak Harnack inequality from
[
27
]
by bounding the
L^{1}
tail by the local infimum of the solution. Such a tail bound is contained in Proposition
11
below, if we consider a solution that is constant in
x
, so that we reduce the hypoelliptic case to a parabolic setting, and if we set
l=0
.
Combining both steps yields the Strong Harnack inequality without any tail term.

Finally we remark that the Strong Harnack inequality fails in the
hypoelliptic
case if the equation is satisfied merely in a bounded velocity domain,
[
25
]
.

1.4.2.
On bounds on the fundamental solution

Regarding the case of
local parabolic
equations, the study of two-sided estimates on the fundamental solution originated with the papers of Nash in 1958
[
38
]
, Aronson in 1967-68
[
1
,
2
]
, and Fabes and Stroock in 1986
[
12
]
.
More precisely, on the one hand, the first proof of Gaussian bounds for linear parabolic second-order equations has been derived by Aronson for uniformly elliptic, but rough, diffusion coefficients in divergence form. Aronson’s derivation is based on energy estimates and Harnack inequalities, and thus exploits the regularity properties of the solution to the underlying equation. On the other hand, the argument of Nash, Fabes and Stroock proves the Gaussian bounds without referring to the regularity properties of the solution, and instead implies estimates on the local behaviour of the solution as a consequence of the Gaussian bounds. When Nash solved Hilbert’s 19th problem on the continuity of solutions to equations with uniformly elliptic rough diffusion coefficients
[
38
]
, he stated without proof estimates on the fundamental solution. These have been revisited by Fabes and Stroock
[
12
]
, who combine Nash’s sketch of the argument together with Davies’ idea of replacing the Euclidean distance by the Riemannian distance associated to the coefficients of the equation at hand
[
7
]
. With this, Fabes-Stroock obtain Gaussian upper and lower bounds, from which they further derive the Harnack inequalities.

Variants of these ideas have been applied to
local kinetic
equations, most notably in
[
33
,
32
]
, or also in the recent preprint
[
3
]
. For
non-local parabolic
equation, the most recent work that we are aware of is by Kassmann-Weidner
[
28
]
, who employ ideas from Aronson’s method to derive a polynomial upper bound on the fundamental solution.

We end this discussion with a historical remark: in 1958 Nash
[
38
]
derived Hölder continuity of parabolic equations in divergence form with rough coefficients, and stated two-sided non-Gaussian estimates for the fundamental solution as a consequence. In 1986, Fabes and Stroock
[
12
]
derived two-sided Gaussian estimates directly from Nash’s ideas, and derived the Harnack inequalities as a consequence. Since we know how to derive the Harnack inequalities for (
1
), one might investigate the derivation of the Harnack inequalities for (
1
) as a consequence of the bounds on the fundamental solution. We assemble this remark into a collection of open problems:

Open Problems 6
.

(i)

Derive the Harnack inequalities of Theorem
9
and Theorem
1
for solutions to (
1
) as a consequence of the polynomial upper and the exponential lower bounds of Theorem
2
and Theorem
3
. Instead of an exponential lower bound, one might need to assume a polynomial lower bound on the fundamental solution. It is also conceivable that it requires the optimal upper bounds.

(ii)

Is the exponential lower bound on the fundamental solution of (
1
) in Theorem
3
optimal for kernels with an average coercivity condition (
15
), an average upper bound (
16
) and with the divergence form symmetry (
17
)? A polynomial lower bound might require a pointwise lower bound on the kernel.

(iii)

Improve the polynomial upper bounds of Theorem
2
, or in other words, make Theorem
4
unconditional. Again, we think this requires a pointwise upper bound on the kernel (
13
) instead of the average upper bound assumed in (
16
).

(iv)

Does the linear
L^{1}
to
L^{\infty}
bound hold for mere
sub-solutions
to (
1
)? The proof method in this article heavily relies on the fact that we work with solutions. However, in the local case, a linear
L^{1}
to
L^{\infty}
bound can be derived even for sub-solutions. It seems to us that the sub-solution property is not sufficient in the non-local case to yield a linear
L^{1}
to
L^{\infty}
bound.

1.5.
Strategy

1.5.1.
For the Strong Harnack inequality

The first step is to derive a tail bound in terms of local quantities: we show that the
L^{1}
norm in time and space of the non-local tail (
14
) is bounded by a local Lebesgue norm. We work on level set functions, so that this step is compatible with the local boundedness result that we aim for in a second step. This introduces two main complications; firstly, the fact that a level set function is not a super-solution of the equation anymore, and secondly, the appearance of cross terms, that is when the test function and the non-locality are on different sides of the level
l
:

\chi_{f>l}(v)\chi_{f<l}(w).

The appearance of these cross terms is a purely non-local effect; in the local case, these terms naturally vanish.
We test the equation with a root of the level set function. Due to the concavity of the test function, this gives rise to a signed term if both the local and the non-local function are above the level. Moreover, on the upper level set, the tail has the good sign as well, since we work with super-solutions. The error terms occur on the lower level set and through the cross terms, both of which exploit the global decay in velocity of the solution. The bound for the cross terms moreover relies on the concavity of the test function, whereas the term that arises when the solution and the test function are both in the lower level set is bounded by an appropriate choice of the cutoff function.

The goal in a second step is to derive a linear
L^{1}
-
L^{\infty}
bound by incorporating the tail bound into the energy estimate and the gain of integrability. This yields local estimates that we can iterate à la De Giorgi. To this end, we work with solutions, which is in contrast to the local case, where a linear
L^{1}
-
L^{\infty}
bound holds for mere sub-solutions.
It might be interesting to investigate whether it is sufficient to work with
sub-solutions
in the non-local case to deduce a linear local boundedness result without tail terms.

1.5.2.
For the bounds on the fundamental solution

As a consequence of the Strong Harnack inequality, Theorem
1
, we adapt Aronson’s method to the non-local kinetic case. In fact, Proposition
5
implies directly an on-diagonal upper bound for the fundamental solution to (
1
). For the off-diagonal bounds, the idea is to construct a function
H
that satisfies

\partial_{t}H+v\cdot\nabla_{x}H+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left[H^{\frac{1}{2}}(w)-H^{\frac{1}{2}}(v)\right]^{2}K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\leq 0.

By testing (
1
) with the product of the solution and this function
H
, we derive an energy estimate on the
L^{2}
norm of
fH^{\frac{1}{2}}
, which in turn permits to determine the decay of the solution from the decay of the function
H
. Together with the on-diagonal bounds that follow as a consequence of the linear
L^{1}
-
L^{\infty}
bound of Proposition
5
, this implies the unconditional and conditional upper bounds on the fundamental solution of Theorem
2
and Theorem
4
.

For
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
, the exponential lower bound of Theorem
3
follows by applying the Strong Harnack inequality of Theorem
1
repeatedly along a path that connects two given points. This yields a pointwise Harnack inequality, which in turn implies an exponential lower bound on any solution that has a non-vanishing average over some local ball. In particular, it implies the exponential bound on the fundamental solution that connects two given points.

The assumptions on the non-local kernel are described in the next subsection.

1.6.
Ellipticity class of the non-local kernel
K

The regularity of integro-differential equations relies on the conditions that are assumed on the kernel of the non-local operator (
2
). A widely studied non-local diffusion operator is the fractional Laplacian (
9
), see
[
9
]
. Based on this, a commonly used assumption is that the kernel is pointwisely bounded from above and below by the kernel of the fractional Laplacian, and satisfies the pointwise symmetry assumptions,
K(v,w)=K(w,v)
and
K(v,v+w)=K(v,v-w)
,
[
6
,
30
,
31
]
. In
[
13
,
26
,
27
]
, a more general class of non-local kernels was considered for which the Weak Harnack inequality is derived. They relax the pointwise upper and lower bounds to conditions in integral form
[
13
]
, and additionally they relax the pointwise symmetry assumptions in
[
26
,
27
]
.

In the case of kinetic equations, the assumptions on the kernel are guided by the Boltzmann equation. Even though the kernel depends in general on time and space we will often omit to write out this dependency for the sake of brevity. We let
0<\lambda_{0}<\Lambda_{0}
and
s\in(0,1)
. Let
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Then we assume that
K
is coercive in the sense that

(15)

\iint_{\Omega}\big(\varphi(v)-\varphi(w)\big)^{2}K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v\geq\lambda_{0}\iint_{\Omega}\frac{\left|\varphi(v)-\varphi(w)\right|^{2}}{\left|v-w\right|^{d+2s}}\,{\rm d}v\,{\rm d}w.

Moreover, we require the following upper bound for
r>0

(16)

\forall v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\quad\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{r}(v)}K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\leq\Lambda_{0}r^{-2s}.

Furthermore, we assume a pointwise divergence form symmetry:

(17)

\forall v,w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\quad K(v,w)=K(w,v).

The local analogue is an operator in divergence form. In case that
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
we assume a pointwise non-divergence form symmetry:

(18)

\forall v,w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\quad K(v,v+w)=K(v,v-w).

This is similarly the non-local analogue of an operator in non-divergence form. The coercivity (
15
) and the upper bound (
16
) are inspired from the series of work by Imbert-Mouhot-Silvestre on the regularity of solutions of the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation conditional to certain macroscopic bounds
[
22
,
23
,
24
,
39
,
20
,
21
]
. For in fact, provided that certain macroscopic quantities remain bounded, the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation (see
[
24
]
) can be understood as an integro-differential equation of the form (
1
), with a kernel satisfying (
15
)-(
16
), and a weaker notion of divergence form symmetry than (
17
), which encodes that on average the non-symmetric part of the kernel is of lower order.
We aim to address the Strong Harnack inequality for the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation in a follow-up article.

1.7.
Invariant transformations

Finally, we introduce the kinetic cylinders that define the domain of solutions to (
1
). Let
f
solve (
1
). Then for
r\in[0,1]
the scaled function
f_{r}(t,x,v)=f(r^{2s}t,r^{1+2s}x,rv)
satisfies

\partial_{t}f_{r}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{r}=\mathcal{L}_{r}f_{r}+h_{r},

where
\mathcal{L}_{r}
is the non-local operator associated to the scaled kernel

K_{r}(t,x,v,w)=r^{d+2s}K(r^{2s}t,r^{1+2s}x,rv,rw),

and the source term is scaled as

h_{r}(t,x,v)=r^{2s}h(r^{2s}t,r^{1+2s}x,rv).

For
r\in[0,1]
the scaled kernel
K_{r}
satisfies (
15
)-(
18
). Moreover,
\left\|h_{r}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{1})}\leq r^{2s}\left\|h\right\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{1})}\leq\left\|h\right\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{1})}
.

Furthermore, the equation is invariant under Galilean transformations, which, we recall, are given by
z\to z_{0}\circ z=(t_{0}+t,x_{0}+x+tv_{0},v_{0}+v)
with
z_{0}=(t_{0},x_{0},v_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
. If
f
is a solution of (
1
) then its Galilean transformation
f_{z_{0}}(z)=f(z_{0}\circ z)
solves

\partial_{t}f_{z_{0}}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{z_{0}}=\mathcal{L}_{z_{0}}f_{z_{0}}+h_{z_{0}},

where
\mathcal{L}_{z_{0}}
is the non-local operator associated to the translated kernel

K_{z_{0}}(t,x,v,w)=K(z_{0}\circ z,v_{0}+w),

and with source

h_{z_{0}}(t,x,v)=h(z_{0}\circ z).

Again the modified kernel
K_{z_{0}}
satisfies (
15
)-(
18
) provided that
K
does, and
h_{z_{0}}
is bounded provided that
h
is.

In view of these invariances we define kinetic cylinders

Q_{r}(z_{0}):=\{(t,x,v):-r^{2s}\leq t-t_{0}\leq 0,\left|v-v_{0}\right|<r,\left|x-x_{0}-(t-t_{0})v_{0}\right|<r^{1+2s}\}

for
r>0
and
z_{0}=(t_{0},x_{0},v_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
. For later reference, we also introduce the cylinder shifted to the past

Q^{-}_{r}(z_{0}):=Q_{r}\big(z_{0}-(2r^{2s},2r^{2s}v_{0},0)\big),

so that in particular for
z_{0}=0

Q^{-}_{r}:=Q_{r}(-2r^{2s},0,0)=(-3r^{2s},-2r^{2s}]\times B_{r^{1+2s}}\times B_{r}.

Similarly the cylinder shifted to the future is denoted as

Q^{+}_{r}(z_{0}):=Q_{r}\big(z_{0}+(2r^{2s},2r^{2s}v_{0},0)\big).

1.8.
Notation

We write
z:=(t,x,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
for a time, space, and velocity variable. We denote by
\mathcal{T}
the transport operator
\mathcal{T}:=\partial_{t}+v\nabla_{x}
.

In general, our domains
Q\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
are cylinders in time, space and velocity. If we slice the cylinder in time, we denote it by
Q^{t}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
, that is
Q^{t}=\left(\{t\}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)\cap Q
.

The positive part of
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
is denoted by
a_{+}=\max\{a,0\}\geq 0
. Correspondingly, we write for the negative part
a_{-}=\min\{a,0\}\leq 0
. In particular, any
a
can be written as
a=a_{+}+a_{-}
.

The bilinear form associated to the non-local operator
\mathcal{L}f
occurring in (
1
) is defined for any
(t,x,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
by

(19)

\mathcal{E}(f,g)=-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\mathcal{L}f(v)g(v)\,{\rm d}v=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big(f(v)-f(w)\big)g(v)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v.

Note that the Dirichlet form associated to a local operator in divergence form has a positive sign.

1.9.
Outline

The paper continues by stating some preliminaries in Section
2
. We then bound the non-local tail on the upper level set in terms of a local Lebesgue norm in Section
3
. Section
4
demonstrates Proposition
5
. We conclude the proof of Theorem
1
in Section
5
. Finally, the upper bound on the fundamental solution is discussed in Section
6
, whereas the lower bound is derived in Section
7
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
The non-local kernel

The results in this subsection are stated in a simplified manner since we are only concerned with symmetric kernels in the sense of (
17
).
Here we summarise two observations on the non-local kernel
K
of (
2
) which are also used later in this paper. The first theorem states the boundedness in
H^{s}
in velocity of the bilinear functional associated to the non-local operator
\mathcal{L}
from (
2
).

Theorem 7

(
H^{s}
estimate
[
22
, Theorem 4.1, Corollary 5.2]
,
[
35
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Let
K:B_{\bar{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a non-negative kernel satisfying (
16
) and (
17
). For any
f,g\in\dot{H}^{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
supported in
B_{\frac{\bar{R}}{2}}
there holds

\mathcal{E}(f,g)\leq\Lambda\left\|f\right\|_{\dot{H}^{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\left\|g\right\|_{\dot{H}^{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})},

where
\mathcal{E}
is the bilinear form (
19
) associated to the non-local operator
\mathcal{L}
of (
2
).

Theorem
7
also motivates our notion of solutions to (
1
).

Definition 1

(Notion of solutions)
.

Assume
0\leq K
satisfies (
15
)-(
17
) and for
\bar{R}>0
, let
(t_{0},t_{1})\times\Omega_{x}:=\big(-(\frac{\bar{R}}{2})^{2s},0\big)\times B_{(\frac{\bar{R}}{2})^{1+2s}}
and
\Omega_{v}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
We say that
f:(t_{0},t_{1})\times\Omega_{x}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a solution of (
1
)-(
2
) in
(t_{0},t_{1})\times\Omega_{x}\times\Omega_{v}
for a source
h\in L^{2}\big((t_{0},t_{1})\times\Omega_{x}\times\Omega_{v}\big)
, if

(i)

f\in C^{0}((t_{0},t_{1}),L^{2}(\Omega_{x}\times\Omega_{v}))\cap L^{2}((t_{0},t_{1})\times\Omega_{x},L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})+H^{s}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
,

(ii)

\mathcal{T}f\in L^{2}((t_{0},t_{1})\times\Omega_{x}\times\Omega_{v})
,

(iii)

f\geq 0
almost everywhere in
(t_{0},t_{1})\times\Omega_{x}\times\Omega_{v}
,

(iv)

f
conserves mass,

(v)

for some
q>d
and some
C_{q}
depending on
q
, we know that
f(t,x,v)\leq C_{q}\langle v\rangle^{-q}
for almost every
(t,x,v)\in[t_{0},t_{1}]\times\Omega_{x}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

(vi)

for all non-negative
\varphi\in L^{2}((t_{0},t_{1})\times\Omega_{x},H^{s}(\Omega_{v}))
such that
\forall(t,x)\in(t_{0},t_{1})\times\Omega_{x}
,
\varphi(t,x,\cdot)
is supported in
\Omega_{v}
, there holds

\int_{(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\times\omega_{x}\times\Omega_{v}}(\mathcal{T}f)\varphi\,{\rm d}z+\int_{(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\int_{\omega_{x}}\mathcal{E}(f,\varphi)\,{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}t-\int_{(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\times\omega_{x}\times\Omega_{v}}h\varphi\,{\rm d}z=0,

for any
t_{0}\leq\tau_{0}\leq\tau_{1}\leq t_{1}
, and any subset
\omega_{x}\subseteq\Omega_{x}
.

Remark 3
.

In fact, in case that
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
, we want to work with a stronger notion of solutions which allows the test function
\varphi
to be in
L^{2}((t_{0},t_{1})\times\Omega_{x},H^{\frac{s}{2}}(\Omega_{v}))
. We argue that there are examples of solutions
f
to (
1
) that satisfy higher differentiability than what would normally be expected from an energy estimate, namely
f\in L^{2}((t_{0},t_{1})\times\Omega_{x},H^{\frac{3s}{2}}(\Omega_{v}))
. This is true for instance if we work with constant coefficients, that is
K(v,w)\sim\left|v-w\right|^{-(d+2s)}
. We don’t know if this is a general principle, but we line out that for the purposes of this article, we can show that test functions in
H^{\frac{s}{2}}(\Omega_{v})
are admissible. The justification is based on a comparison principle involving the fundamental solution to the equation with constant coefficients, which can be found in Appendix
A
. We argue that qualitatively the solution is in
H^{\frac{3s}{2}}(\Omega_{v})
, so that we can test the equation with a function in
H^{\frac{s}{2}}(\Omega_{v})
, which does not affect the final results in this paper quantitatively.

We emphasise this for the purpose of the tail estimate in Proposition
11
, where we use a test function which is merely in
H^{\frac{\bar{s}}{2}}(\Omega_{v})
for any
\bar{s}\in(0,1)
.

The second observation is a non-local analogue of the product rule.

Lemma 8

(Commutator estimates
[
22
, Lemmata 4.10, 4.11]
)
.

Let
K
be a non-negative kernel satisfying (
16
). Let
D
be a closed set and
\Omega
open such that
D\Subset\Omega\subset B_{\bar{R}/2}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
for
0<\bar{R}\leq 2
. Let
\varphi
be a smooth function with support in
D
, let
f\in H^{s}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and let
\rho=\frac{\textrm{dist}(D,{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus\Omega)}{2}
. We write

\mathcal{L}[\varphi f]-\varphi\mathcal{L}f=h_{1}+h_{2},

where
h_{1},h_{2}
are given by

\displaystyle h_{1}(v)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{\rho}(v)}f(w)\big(\varphi(w)-\varphi(v)\big)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w,

\displaystyle h_{2}(v)=\int_{B_{\rho}(v)}f(w)\big(\varphi(w)-\varphi(v)\big)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w.

Then, by construction
h_{2}=0
outside
\Omega
, and there holds

(20)

\left\|h_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus\Omega)}\leq\Lambda\rho^{-2s}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(D)}.

Moreover, if
s\in(0,1/2)
, there holds

(21)

\left\|h_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq\Lambda\rho^{1-2s}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{1}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

Else if
s\in[1/2,1)
and if additionally
K
satisfies (
17
), then there exists
h_{22},h_{23}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
such that

h_{2}=h_{22}+(-\Delta_{v})^{\frac{s}{2}}h_{23},

with

(22)

\displaystyle\left\|h_{22}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\rho^{2-2s}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{2}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\Lambda\rho^{1-s}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{1}}\left\|f\right\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle\left\|h_{23}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\rho^{1-s}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{1}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

We reprove this lemma to make the dependence on
\rho
in (
21
) and (
22
) precise. We need it for the optimal exponent on the radius in Proposition
5
.

Proof.

We let
E=D+B_{\rho}
so that
D\Subset E\Subset\Omega
with
\textrm{dist}(D,{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus E)=\rho
and
\textrm{dist}(E,{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus\Omega)=\rho
.

\rho
\rho
v
1
0
D
E
\Omega
\varphi

Figure 2.
Illustration of the smooth cutoff
\varphi
from Lemma
8
with which we commute
f
, and the supports of the cutoff and the local contribution of the commutator. Note
D=\textrm{supp }\varphi
, and
E=\textrm{supp }h_{2}
.

To bound
h_{1}
, we notice that
\varphi(v)=0
for
v\notin D
. Thus if
v\notin\Omega\supset D
, then

h_{1}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{\rho}(v)}f(w)\varphi(w)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w=\int_{D}f(w)\varphi(w)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w.

Therefore, using Cauchy-Schwarz, (
16
) and Fubini’s theorem

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus\Omega}h_{1}^{2}\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus\Omega}\Bigg(\int_{D}f(w)\varphi(w)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)^{2}\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\varphi\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus\Omega}\Bigg(\int_{D}f^{2}(w)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)\Bigg(\int_{D}K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\rho^{-2s}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\int_{D}f^{2}(w)\int_{\left|v-w\right|\geq 2\rho}K(v,w)\,{\rm d}v\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{2}\rho^{-4s}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(D)}^{2}.

This yields (
20
).

To bound
h_{2}
we first consider
s\in(0,1/2)
. We use Cauchy-Schwarz, (
16
) for
s<1/2
and Fubini

\displaystyle\left\|h_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\Bigg(\int_{B_{\rho}(v)}f(w)\big(\varphi(w)-\varphi(v)\big)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)^{2}\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq\int_{E}\Bigg(\int_{B_{\rho}(v)}f^{2}(w)\left|\varphi(w)-\varphi(v)\right|K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)

\displaystyle\times\Bigg(\int_{B_{\rho}(v)}\left|\varphi(w)-\varphi(v)\right|K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{1}}^{2}\int_{E}\Bigg(\int_{B_{\rho}(v)}f^{2}(w)\left|v-w\right|K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)

\displaystyle\times\Bigg(\int_{B_{\rho}(v)}\left|v-w\right|K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\rho^{1-2s}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{1}}^{2}\int_{\Omega}f^{2}(w)\int_{E\cap B_{\rho}(w)}\left|v-w\right|K(v,w)\,{\rm d}v\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{2}\rho^{2-4s}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{1}}^{2}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}.

This yields (
21
).

Second we consider
s\in[1/2,1)
. Due to (
17
), we find

\displaystyle h_{2}=\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\rho}(v)}f(w)\big(\varphi(w)-\varphi(v)\big)\big(K(v,w)+K(w,v)\big)\,{\rm d}w}_{=:h_{2}^{\textrm{sym}}}.

We estimate
h_{2}^{\textrm{sym}}
by duality. Let
g\in H^{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. Then, since
\textrm{supp }h_{2}^{\textrm{sym}}\subseteq E
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{E}h_{2}^{\textrm{sym}}(v)g(v)\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\int_{B_{\rho}(v)}g(v)f(w)\big(\varphi(w)-\varphi(v)\big)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega\cap\left|v-w\right|<\rho}f(v)\big(g(v)-g(w)\big)\big(\varphi(w)-\varphi(v)\big)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega\cap\left|v-w\right|<\rho}g(v)\big(f(w)-f(v)\big)\big(\varphi(w)-\varphi(v)\big)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v.

Thus by Cauchy-Schwarz, (
16
) and Theorem
7

\displaystyle\int_{E}h_{2}^{\textrm{sym}}(v)g(v)\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\Bigg\{\int_{\Omega}\Bigg(\int_{\Omega\cap\left|v-w\right|<\rho}\big(g(v)-g(w)\big)\big(\varphi(w)-\varphi(v)\big)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)^{2}\,{\rm d}v\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|g\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\Bigg\{\int_{\Omega}\Bigg(\int_{\Omega\cap\left|v-w\right|<\rho}\big(f(w)-f(v)\big)\big(\varphi(w)-\varphi(v)\big)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)^{2}\,{\rm d}v\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\Bigg\{\int_{\Omega}\Bigg(\int_{\Omega\cap\left|v-w\right|<\rho}\big(g(v)-g(w)\big)^{2}K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\Bigg(\int_{\Omega\cap\left|v-w\right|<\rho}\left|\varphi(w)-\varphi(v)\right|^{2}K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)\,{\rm d}v\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|g\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\Bigg\{\int_{\Omega}\Bigg(\int_{\Omega\cap\left|v-w\right|<\rho}\big(f(w)-f(v)\big)^{2}K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\Bigg(\int_{\Omega\cap\left|v-w\right|<\rho}\left|\varphi(w)-\varphi(v)\right|^{2}K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\Bigg)\,{\rm d}v\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho^{1-s}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{1}}\Bigg\{\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega\cap\left|v-w\right|<\rho}\big(g(v)-g(w)\big)^{2}K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho^{1-s}\left\|g\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{1}}\Bigg\{\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega\cap\left|v-w\right|<\rho}\big(f(w)-f(v)\big)^{2}K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\rho^{1-s}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{1}}\Big(\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\left\|g\right\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}+\left\|g\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\left\|f\right\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}\Big).

This estimate implies that there exists
h_{22},h_{23}
such that

h_{2}=h_{2}^{\textrm{sym}}=h_{22}^{\textrm{sym}}+(-\Delta_{v})^{\frac{s}{2}}h_{23}^{\textrm{sym}},

with

\displaystyle\left\|h_{22}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq\Lambda\rho^{1-s}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{1}}\left\|f\right\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)},\qquad\left\|h_{23}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq\Lambda\rho^{1-s}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{1}}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

∎

2.2.
Weak Harnack

We make use of the Weak Harnack inequality derived in
[
22
, Theorem 1.6]
and
[
35
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Theorem 9

(Weak Harnack inequality
[
22
, Thm. 1.6]
,
[
35
, Thm. 1.1]
)
.

Let
f
be a non-negative super-solution to (
1
)-(
2
) in
[-3,0]\times Q_{1}^{t}:=[-3,0]\times B_{1}\times B_{1}
with a non-negative kernel
K
satisfying (
15
)-(
17
).
Then there is
C>0
and
\zeta>0
depending on
s,d,\lambda,\Lambda
such that for
r_{0}<\frac{1}{3}
the Weak Harnack inequality is satisfied:

(23)

\Bigg(\int_{\tilde{Q}_{\frac{r_{0}}{2}}^{-}}f^{\zeta}\,{\rm d}z\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{\zeta}}\leq C\Big(\inf_{Q_{\frac{r_{0}}{2}}}f+\left\|h\right\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{1})}\Big),

where
\tilde{Q}_{\frac{r_{0}}{2}}^{-}:=Q_{\frac{r_{0}}{2}}\big((-\frac{5}{2}r_{0}^{2s}+\frac{1}{2}(\frac{r_{0}}{2})^{2s},0,0)\big)
, see Figure
1
.

The proof of the Weak Harnack inequality follows the De Giorgi method: in a first step one shows the local gain of regularity from
L^{2}
to
L^{\infty}
, then in a second step from
L^{\infty}
to
C^{\alpha}
. The Weak Harnack inequality follows in the end from the
C^{\alpha}
regularity by using a covering argument. We refer the reader to
[
22
]
for a proof that is constructive in case that
s\in(0,1/2)
and based on compactness arguments in case that
s\geq 1/2
; or to
[
35
]
for a quantitative proof for any
s\in(0,1)
.

2.3.
Iteration argument

Finally, we use a standard iteration argument, see for example
[
14
, Lemma 8.18]
.

Lemma 10
.

Let
\phi:[0,T]\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a non-negative bounded function. Suppose that for
0\leq r<R\leq T
we have

\phi(r)\leq A(R-r)^{-\alpha}+\delta\phi(R)

for some
A,\alpha>0
,
0\leq\delta<1
. Then there exists a constant
c
depending on
\alpha,\delta
such that for
0\leq r<R\leq T
we have

\phi(r)\leq cA(R-r)^{-\alpha}.

3.
Tail bound on upper level sets

In this section we derive the first step to the Strong Harnack inequality: we show a bound on the non-local tail term in
L^{1}
by a local quantity. The idea, inspired from
[
29
]
, is to test the equation against
f^{-(1-\zeta)}
for
\zeta\in(0,1)
and where
f>0
solves (
1
), with the aim of obtaining a local bound on the
L^{1}
norm of the tail (
14
).
If we work with super-solutions, then the tail has the good sign just by virtue of the definition of
\mathcal{L}
(
2
). Moreover, for
\zeta<1
, the concavity of the test function gives a signed term. Finally, if we work with non-negative super-solutions, then we end up with the local tail bound in
L^{1}
.
We then want to incorporate this
L^{1}
tail bound in the next step into the derivation of a local boundedness result. However, this boundedness result is established on level set functions in the spirit of De Giorgi. Even though
f-l
is a solution to (
1
) for any
l\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, the positive part
(f-l)_{+}
is merely a sub-solution. The tail bound in this section, however, requires a super-solution. Thus, in order to exploit the
L^{1}
tail bound directly in the energy estimate (
48
), we test with the level set function to some inverse power, that is
(f-l)_{+}^{-(1-\zeta)}
for any
l\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and for the sake of obtaining a local bound, we localise with some cutoff.
The non-locality in velocity introduces cross terms, that is terms when
f(v)
and
f(w)
are on different sides of the level. These are dealt with by exploiting the polynomial velocity decay of the super-solution. Moreover, the term that arises when both, the function and the test function, are below level
l
, can be made sufficiently small by appropriately twisting the cutoff function on the lower level set: its derivative has to be sufficiently small.

The appearance of cross terms is a purely non-local effect. In order to use the velocity decay of the solution in the whole space, we need to assume (
1
) to be satisfied for all
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

Proposition 11

(Non-local to local bound)
.

Let
R>0
,
z_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
, and denote by
[t_{1},t_{2}]\times\Omega_{x}
the set
[t_{1},t_{2}]\times\Omega_{x}:=[-R^{2s}+t_{0},t_{0}]\times B_{R^{1+2s}}(x_{0}+(t-t_{0})v_{0})
. For a given
l\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and for any non-negative super-solution
f
of (
1
) in
[t_{1},t_{2}]\times\Omega_{x}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with a non-negative source term
h
, such that
f(t,x,v)\leq C_{q}\langle v\rangle^{-q}
for almost every
(t,x,v)\in[t_{1},t_{2}]\times\Omega_{x}\times\Omega_{v}
for
q>d
; and such that the non-negative kernel satisfies (
16
)-(
18
), we can find for any
\zeta\in\left(0,1\right)
, a constant
C>0
depending on
\zeta,s,d,\Lambda_{0},C_{q}
such that,
in case that
l=0
,

(24)

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{\frac{R}{2}}}

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{R}}(f-l)_{+}(w)K(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}z

\displaystyle\leq CR^{n(1-\zeta)-2s}\left(\sup_{Q_{R}}(f-l)_{+}\right)^{1-\zeta}\left(\int_{Q_{R}}(f-l)_{+}\,{\rm d}z\right)^{\zeta};

and in case that
l>0
,

(25)

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{\frac{R}{2}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{R}}

\displaystyle(f-l)_{+}(w)\chi_{f>l}(v)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq CR_{0}^{n(1-\zeta)-2s}\mathcal{M}_{l}\left|\big\{f>l\big\}\cap Q_{R_{0}}\right|^{\zeta},

where

(26)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{l}

\displaystyle:=\left[1+\left(\frac{\sup_{Q_{R_{0}}}(f-l)_{+}}{l}\right)^{\zeta}+\left(\frac{\sup_{Q_{R_{0}}}(f-l)_{+}}{l}\right)^{1-\zeta}\right]\left(l+\sup_{Q_{R_{0}}}(f-l)_{+}\right).

and where
R_{0}=2\max\left\{R,\left(\frac{C_{q}}{l}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\right\}
with
l>0
.

Remark 4
.

i.

For
l=0
we could relate the local
L^{\zeta}
norm on the right hand side to the local infimum using the Weak Harnack inequality of Theorem
9
, if we additionally assume (
15
). Moreover, for
l=0
, it would suffice to suppose (
1
) to hold on a bounded subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

For the proof, we collect preliminary estimates in the following lemma.

Lemma 12
.

Let
R>0
and
v_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Let
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
be some smooth function with support in
B_{R}(v_{0})
. Let
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be non-negative,
l\geq 0
,
f_{l,\varepsilon}=(f-l)_{+}+\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon>0
and
\zeta\in(0,1)
.
Then there holds:

i.

First, we have for any
v,w
outside
f(v)\leq l\cap f(w)\leq l

(27)

\displaystyle\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]

\displaystyle\Big[f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(w)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{4(1-\zeta)}{\zeta^{2}}\frac{\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]}{\big[f_{l,\varepsilon}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}(w)\big]}\Big(f^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}_{l,\varepsilon}(v)-f^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}_{l,\varepsilon}(w)\Big)^{2}.

ii.

Second, for any
v,w
outside
f(v)\leq l\cap f(w)\leq l

(28)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\min\Big\{f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v),f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(w)\Big\}\left|f(v)-f(w)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\zeta}\frac{\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]}{\big[f_{l,\varepsilon}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}(w)\big]}\max\left\{f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v),f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\right\}\left|f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\right|.

iii.

Third,

(29)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left|\left(\eta f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}\right)(v)-\left(\eta f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}\right)(w)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle-\big(\eta(v)-\eta(w)\big)^{2}f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\zeta}(w)

\displaystyle\leq\min\big\{\eta^{2}(v),\eta^{2}(w)\big\}\left|f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\right|^{2}.

and

(30)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\max\big\{\eta^{2}(v),\eta^{2}(w)\big\}\left|f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left|\left(\eta f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}\right)(v)-\left(\eta f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}\right)(w)\right|^{2}+2\big(\eta(v)-\eta(w)\big)^{2}f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\zeta}(v).

Proof of Lemma
12
.

i.

We define for
\xi>0

F(\xi):=\xi^{-(1-\zeta)},

so that

F^{\prime}(\xi)=-(1-\zeta)\xi^{-(2-\zeta)}\leq 0.

Then we compute using Jensen’s inequality for all
v,w
outside
f(v)\leq l\cap f(w)\leq l

\displaystyle\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]

\displaystyle\Big[f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(w)\Big]

\displaystyle=\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\Big[F\big(f_{l,\varepsilon}(v)\big)-F\big(f_{l,\varepsilon}(w)\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\int_{f_{l,\varepsilon}(w)}^{f_{l,\varepsilon}(v)}F^{\prime}(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=-(1-\zeta)\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\int^{f_{l,\varepsilon}(v)}_{f_{l,\varepsilon}(w)}\xi^{-(2-\zeta)}\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle\leq-(1-\zeta)\frac{\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]}{\big[f_{l,\varepsilon}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}(w)\big]}\Bigg(\int^{f_{l,\varepsilon}(v)}_{f_{l,\varepsilon}(w)}\xi^{-\frac{(2-\zeta)}{2}}\,{\rm d}\xi\Bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4(1-\zeta)}{\zeta^{2}}\frac{\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]}{\big[f_{l,\varepsilon}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}(w)\big]}\Big(f^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}_{l,\varepsilon}(v)-f^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}_{l,\varepsilon}(w)\Big)^{2}.

This proves (
27
).

ii.

To prove (
28
) we denote by

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(\xi):=\sqrt{-F^{\prime}(\xi)}=(1-\zeta)^{\frac{1}{2}}\xi^{-\frac{(2-\zeta)}{2}},

so that

\mathcal{F}(\xi)=\frac{2(1-\zeta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\zeta}\xi^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}.

Then, for all
v,w
outside
f(v)\leq l\cap f(w)\leq l
, we note by Cauchy’s mean value theorem

(31)

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta}{2(1-\zeta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\frac{\left|f(v)-f(w)\right|}{\Big|f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\Big|}=\frac{\left|f(v)-f(w)\right|}{\left|\mathcal{F}\big(f_{l,\varepsilon}(v)\big)-\mathcal{F}\big(f_{l,\varepsilon}(w)\big)\right|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left|f(v)-f(w)\right|}{\left|f_{l,\varepsilon}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}(w)\right|}\max\Bigg\{\frac{1}{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(f_{l,\varepsilon}(v))},\frac{1}{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(f_{l,\varepsilon}(w))}\Bigg\}.

Upon multiplying (
31
) by
\min\big\{f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v),f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(w)\big\}
we deduce

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta}{2}

\displaystyle\min\Big\{f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v),f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(w)\Big\}\frac{\left|f(v)-f(w)\right|}{\Big|f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\Big|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left|f(v)-f(w)\right|}{\left|f_{l,\varepsilon}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}(w)\right|}\max\Bigg\{f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{(2-\zeta)}{2}}(v),f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{(2-\zeta)}{2}}(w)\Bigg\}\min\Big\{f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v),f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(w)\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left|f(v)-f(w)\right|}{\left|f_{l,\varepsilon}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}(w)\right|}\max\Bigg\{f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v),f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\Bigg\},

or equivalently (
28
).

iii.

To prove (
29
) we assume with no loss in generality that
\eta(v)\leq\eta(w)
. Then we simply write

\displaystyle\eta(v)f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v)-\eta(w)f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)=\eta(v)\Big(f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\Big)+f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\Big(\eta(v)-\eta(w)\Big).

This implies

\displaystyle\Big|\eta(v)f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v)-\eta(w)f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\Big|^{2}\leq 2\eta^{2}(v)\Big|f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\Big|^{2}+2f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\zeta}(w)\Big(\eta(v)-\eta(w)\Big)^{2}.

Similarly, to prove (
30
) we note

\eta(w)\Big(f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\Big)=\eta(v)f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v)-\eta(w)f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)+\Big(\eta(w)-\eta(v)\Big)f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v),

so that

\eta^{2}(w)\Big|f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\Big|^{2}\leq 2\Big|\eta(v)f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(v)-\eta(w)f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\frac{\zeta}{2}}(w)\Big|^{2}+2\Big(\eta(w)-\eta(v)\Big)^{2}f_{l,\varepsilon}^{\zeta}(v).

∎

Proof of Proposition
11
.

Our aim is to get a local bound on the tail on the upper level set. To this end we do a concavity estimate on the upper level sets by testing the equation with the level set function of the solution to some inverse power. This creates
cross
terms. These are dealt with by exploiting the polynomial decay of the super-solution.

With no loss in generality, we may assume
h=0
. Indeed, if
h\geq 0
, then
f
is a super-solution to (
1
) with
h=0
as well. Moreover, due to the translation invariance, we may, with no loss in generality, consider
z_{0}=(0,0,0)
.

As discussed in Remark
3
, the justification of the computations below in case that
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
relies on a comparison of the solution for (
1
) to a solution of the fractional Kolmogorov equation, and quantifying the loss of regularity that the roughness of the coefficient introduces. The solution can be represented through the fundamental solution of the fractional Kolmogorov equation for an appropriate source term. We show that we can work with a solution that enjoys sufficient differentiability in order for the test function constructed below to be admissible. In the limit, where the smoothening of the fractional Laplacian is lost, this higher regularity vanishes as well. But only once the statement of Proposition
11
is proven for more regular solutions, we pass to the limit, which solves (
1
). Details are carried out in the Appendix
A
. For
s<\frac{1}{2}
this argument is obsolete.

For
l\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
we consider
\psi_{l}\geq 0
given by

(32)

\psi_{l}(t,x,v):=\eta^{2}(t,x,v)\chi_{f>l}(t,x,v)+\chi_{f<l}(t,x,v)\xi(v),

where
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d+1})
with
0\leq\eta\leq 1
such that
\eta=1
in
Q_{\frac{3R}{4}}
and
\eta=0
outside
Q_{\frac{7R}{8}}
; moreover,
\xi
is a cutoff in
v
such that
\xi=1
in
B_{r}
and
\xi=0
outside
B_{\bar{R}}
with
r,\bar{R}

to be determined.

With no loss in generality, we assume there is at least one point
z_{0}
in
Q_{R}
where
f(z_{0})>l
. Otherwise the statement (
25
) is trivially satisfied.

Step 1: Non-local operator.

We first consider the non-local operator
\mathcal{E}
for fixed
(t,x)
.
Due to the divergence structure of
K
(
17
), we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\left(f,\psi f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\psi_{l}(v)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\Big[\big(f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}\psi_{l}\big)(v)-\big(f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}\psi_{l}\big)(w)\Big]

\displaystyle\times\big[K(v,w)+K(w,v)\big]\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v.

We then split
\mathcal{E}
into three parts: we distinguish when
f(v),f(w)>l
, when
f(v),f(w)<l
and when either
f(v)>l>f(w)
or
f(w)>l>f(v)
. We write

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\left(f,\psi f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\psi_{l}(v)K(v,w)\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\psi_{l}(v)K(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\psi_{l}(v)K(v,w)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(w)<l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\psi_{l}(v)K(v,w)\big[\chi_{f(v)>l>f(w)}+\chi_{f(w)>l>f(v)}\big]\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=:\mathcal{E}_{up}+\mathcal{E}_{low}+\mathcal{E}_{cross}.

The indicator functions that we added respect the symmetry of each integrand.

Step 1-i: Non-locality on the lower level set.

We find writing
K^{sym}(v,w):=K(v,w)+K(w,v)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{low}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\Big[\big(f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}\psi_{l}\big)(v)-\big(f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}\psi_{l}\big)(w)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times K^{sym}(v,w)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(w)<l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{-(1-\zeta)}}{4}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\Big[\xi(v)-\xi(w)\Big]K^{sym}(v,w)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(w)<l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{-(1-\zeta)}}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(v)\Big[\xi(v)-\xi(w)\Big]K^{sym}(v,w)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(w)<l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{-(1-\zeta)}}{2}\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(v)\Big[\xi(v)-\xi(w)\Big]K^{sym}(v,w)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(w)<l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v,

where the last inequality uses the definition of
\xi
.

Due to (
16
), we have for
\left|v-w\right|>\bar{R}-r

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}

\displaystyle\int_{\left|v-w\right|>\bar{R}-r}f(v)\Big[\xi(v)-\xi(w)\Big]\big[K(v,w)+K(w,v)\big]\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(w)<l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}\int_{\left|v-w\right|>\bar{R}-r}f(v)\xi(v)\big[K(v,w)+K(w,v)\big]\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(w)<l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq C(\bar{R}-r)^{-2s}\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}f(v)\,{\rm d}v.

For
\left|v-w\right|<\bar{R}-r
we Taylor-expand
\xi
, in case that
s<\frac{1}{2}
up to first order, in case that
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
up to second order. We start with
s<\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}

\displaystyle\int_{\left|v-w\right|<\bar{R}-r}f(v)\Big[\xi(v)-\xi(w)\Big]\big[K(v,w)+K(w,v)\big]\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(w)<l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq\left\|D\xi\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}f(v)\int_{\left|v-w\right|<\bar{R}-r}\left|v-w\right|\big[K(v,w)+K(w,v)\big]\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq C(\bar{R}-r)^{1-2s}(\bar{R}-r)^{-1}\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}f(v)\,{\rm d}v,

using that
\left\|D\xi\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\sim(\bar{R}-r)^{-1}
, and (
16
).

For
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
, we need to do a further distinction. We abbreviate
K^{sym}(v,w)=K(v,w)+K(w,v)
, so that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}\int_{\left|v-w\right|<\bar{R}-r}f(v)\Big[\xi(v)-\xi(w)\Big]K^{sym}(v,w)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(w)<l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}\int_{\left|z\right|<\bar{R}-r}f(v)\Big[\xi(v)-\xi(v+z)\Big]K^{sym}(v,v+z)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(v+z)<l}\,{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}\int_{\left|z\right|<\bar{R}-r}f(v)\Big[\xi(v)-\xi(v+z)\Big]K^{sym}(v,v+z)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(v+z)<l}\chi_{f(v-z)<l}\,{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle+\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}\int_{\left|z\right|<\bar{R}-r}f(v)\Big[\xi(v)-\xi(v+z)\Big]K^{sym}(v,v+z)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(v+z)<l}\chi_{f(v-z)>l}\,{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}v.

We Taylor expand the first term, and use the symmetries (
17
) and (
18
), due to which
K^{sym}(v,v+z)=K^{sym}(v,v-z)
,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}

\displaystyle\int_{\left|z\right|<\bar{R}-r}f(v)\Big[\xi(v)-\xi(v+z)\Big]K^{sym}(v,v+z)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(v+z)<l}\chi_{f(v-z)<l}\,{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}f(v)D\xi(v)\chi_{f(v)<l}\int_{\left|z\right|<\bar{R}-r}(-z)K^{sym}(v,v+z)\chi_{f(v+z)<l}\chi_{f(v-z)<l}\,{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle+\left\|D^{2}\xi\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}f(v)\chi_{f(v)<l}\int_{\left|z\right|<\bar{R}-r}\left|z\right|^{2}K^{sym}(v,v+z)\chi_{f(v+z)<l}\chi_{f(v-z)<l}\,{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq C(\bar{R}-r)^{-2}(\bar{R}-r)^{2-2s}\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}f(v)\,{\rm d}v.

For the second term, we use the fact that on the set
\{f(v-z)>l\}
we have
l<f(v-z)\leq C_{q}\langle v-z\rangle^{-q}
, so that
\left|v-z\right|\leq\left(\frac{C_{q}}{l}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}
. Keeping this in mind, we find

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}\int_{\left|z\right|<\bar{R}-r}f(v)\Big[\xi(v)-\xi(v+z)\Big]K^{sym}(v,v+z)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(v+z)<l}\chi_{f(v-z)>l}\,{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}\int_{\left|z\right|<\bar{R}-r}f(v)\big[\xi(v)-1\big]K^{sym}(v,v+z)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(v+z)<l}\chi_{f(v-z)>l}\,{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}\int_{\left|z\right|<\bar{R}-r}f(v)\big[1-\xi(v+z)\big]K^{sym}(v,v+z)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(v+z)<l}\chi_{f(v-z)>l}\,{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}v

using that
\xi\leq 1

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}\int_{\left|z\right|<\bar{R}-r}f(v)\big[1-\xi(v+z)\big]K^{sym}(v,v+z)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(v+z)<l}\chi_{f(v-z)>l}\,{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}v.

But
1-\xi(v+z)=0
for
\left|v+z\right|<r
. Thus
\left|v+z\right|\geq r
and if we pick
r\geq 2\left(\frac{C_{q}}{l}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}
, then
\left|v-z\right|<\frac{r}{2}
, so that

\left|z\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\left|v+z\right|-\left|v-z\right|\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(r-\frac{r}{2}\right)=\frac{r}{4}.

Therefore, we find

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}\int_{\left|z\right|<\bar{R}-r}f(v)\big[1-\xi(v+z)\big]K^{sym}(v,v+z)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(v+z)<l}\chi_{f(v-z)>l}\,{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}\int_{\frac{r}{4}<\left|z\right|<\bar{R}-r}f(v)\big[1-\xi(v+z)\big]K^{sym}(v,v+z)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(v+z)<l}\chi_{f(v-z)>l}\,{\rm d}z\,{\rm d}v

using
w=v-z
, Fubini, bounding
1-\xi\leq 1
, and using
\left|v-w\right|=\left|2v-w-v\right|=\left|v-(2v-w)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\left|w\right|<\frac{r}{2}}\int_{\left|v\right|\leq\bar{R},\frac{r}{4}<\left|v-w\right|<\bar{R}-r}f(v)K^{sym}(v,2v-w)\chi_{f(v)<l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\,{\rm d}v\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\leq Clr^{-2s}\int_{\left|w\right|<\frac{r}{2}}\chi_{f(w)>l}\,{\rm d}w.

We conclude

(33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{low}\leq C\varepsilon^{-(1-\zeta)}(\bar{R}-r)^{-2s}\int_{B_{\bar{R}}}f(v)\,{\rm d}v+C\varepsilon^{-(1-\zeta)}lr^{-2s}\left|\{f>l\}\cap B_{\frac{r}{2}}\right|.

Step 1-ii: Cross non-locality.

For the cross term, we note that
l>0
. If
l=0
, then
\chi_{f(v)>l>f(w)}+\chi_{f(w)>l>f(v)}=0
due to the non-negativity of
f
. We find

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{cross}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\Big[\big(f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}\psi_{l}\big)(v)-\big(f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}\psi_{l}\big)(w)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\big[K(v,w)+K(w,v)\big]\big[\chi_{f(v)>l>f(w)}+\chi_{f(w)>l>f(v)}\big]\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\Big[\big(f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}\psi_{l}\big)(v)-\big(f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}\psi_{l}\big)(w)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\big[K(v,w)+K(w,v)\big]\chi_{f(v)>l>f(w)}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\Big[\eta^{2}(v)(f-l+\varepsilon)^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)-\varepsilon^{-(1-\zeta)}\xi(w)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\big[K(v,w)+K(w,v)\big]\chi_{f(v)>l>f(w)}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\Big[\eta^{2}(v)(f-l+\varepsilon)^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)-\varepsilon^{-(1-\zeta)}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\big[K(v,w)+K(w,v)\big]\chi_{f(v)>l>f(w)}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\varepsilon^{-(1-\zeta)}}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\big[1-\xi(w)\big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\big[K(v,w)+K(w,v)\big]\chi_{f(v)>l>f(w)}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v.

Due to the concavity of the test function, the first part of the cross term has a sign. For the second part we use the fact that
f(v)\leq C_{q}\langle v\rangle^{-q}
for any
q>0
, so that on
\{f(v)>l\}
we have
\left|v\right|<\langle v\rangle<\left(\frac{C_{q}}{l}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}
. At the same time,
(1-\xi)(w)=0
for
\left|w\right|\leq r
, so if we pick
r\geq 2\left(\frac{C_{q}}{l}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}
then
\left|v-w\right|>\frac{r}{2}
. We therefore find

(34)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{cross}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{-(1-\zeta)}}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{\left|v-w\right|>\frac{r}{2}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\big[1-\xi(w)\big]

\displaystyle\times\big[K(v,w)+K(w,v)\big]\chi_{f(v)>l>f(w)}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{-(1-\zeta)}}{2}\int_{B_{\frac{r}{2}}}\int_{\left|v-w\right|>\frac{r}{2}}\big[(f-l)_{+}(v)+l\big]

\displaystyle\times\big[K(v,w)+K(w,v)\big]\chi_{f(v)>l>f(w)}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{-(1-\zeta)}}{2}\left[\left(\frac{r}{2}\right)^{-2s}\int_{B_{\frac{r}{2}}}(f-l)_{+}(v)\,{\rm d}v+l\left|\{f>l\}\cap B_{\frac{r}{2}}\right|\right].

Step 1-iii: Non-locality on the upper level set.

It remains to bound
\mathcal{E}_{up}
.
For fixed time and space, we find

(35)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{up}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\eta^{2}(v)K(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\eta^{2}(v)K(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle+\int_{B_{R}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{R}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\eta^{2}(v)K(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=:\mathcal{E}_{up}^{loc}+\mathcal{E}_{up}^{tail}.

For
\mathcal{E}^{tail}_{up}
, we note that there is no singularity, due to the choice of
\eta
, which vanishes outside
B_{\frac{7R}{8}}
. Therefore, by (
16
)

(36)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{up}^{tail}

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{R}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{R}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\eta^{2}(v)K(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{R}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{R}}(f-l)_{+}(v)f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\eta^{2}(v)K(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle-\int_{B_{R}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{R}}(f-l)_{+}(w)f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\eta^{2}(v)K(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{0}\left(\frac{R}{8}\right)^{-2s}\int_{B_{R}}((f-l)_{+}+\varepsilon)^{\zeta}(v)\chi_{f>l}(v)\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle-\int_{B_{R}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{R}}(f-l)_{+}(w)f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\eta^{2}(v)K(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v.

In particular, the tail of
(f-l)_{+}
has a negative sign.

We now abbreviate
K^{sym}(v,w):=K(v,w)+K(w,v)
. Then we find for the local contribution of
\mathcal{E}_{up}

(37)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{up}^{loc}

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\eta^{2}(v)K(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\Big[f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\eta^{2}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(w)\eta^{2}(w)\Big]

\displaystyle\times K^{sym}(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\Big[f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\eta^{2}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(w)\eta^{2}(w)\Big]

\displaystyle\times K^{sym}(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\big[\chi_{f(v)>f(w)}+\chi_{f(w)>f(v)}\big]\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\Big[f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)\eta^{2}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(w)\eta^{2}(w)\Big]

\displaystyle\times K^{sym}(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\chi_{f(w)>f(v)}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]\Big[f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(v)-f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(w)\Big]\eta^{2}(v)

\displaystyle\times K^{sym}(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\chi_{f(w)>f(v)}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\big[f(v)-f(w)\big]f_{l,\varepsilon}^{-(1-\zeta)}(w)\big[\eta^{2}(v)-\eta^{2}(w)\big]

\displaystyle\times K^{sym}(v,w)\chi_{f(v)>l}\chi_{f(w)>l}\chi_{f(w)>f(v)}\,{\rm d}w\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=:I_{sign}+I_{sym}.

The term
I_{sign}
has a sign due to the concavity of the test function, whereas in
I_{sym}
the cutoff
\eta
absorbs some of the singularity of the kernel
K
.
To quantitatively bound
I_{sign}
and
I_{sym}
we use the estimates of Lemma
12
. For
I_{sign}
we use (
